**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

```c
void s244_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c, float* d) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Store original a[i+1] values to break dependency
        float a_next_old = a[1];
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D-1; ++i) {
            // Compute new b[i] first (needed for a[i+1] update)
            float b_new = c[i] + b[i];
            
            // Update a[i] using original b[i]
            a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];
            
            // Update b[i] with precomputed value
            b[i] = b_new;
            
            // Update a[i+1] using stored old value
            float a_next_temp = a_next_old;
            if (i < LEN_1D-2) {
                a_next_old = a[i+2];
            }
            a[i+1] = b_new + a_next_temp * d[i];
        }
    }
}

## Analysis

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code
The optimized code compiles without errors (as indicated by the compiler feedback showing remarks, not errors).

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases

**Test Case 1**: LEN_1D=3, iterations=1
Initial values:
- a = [1, 2, 3]
- b = [4, 5, 6]
- c = [7, 8, 9]
- d = [0.1, 0.2, 0.3]

**Original code execution**:
- i=0: a[0] = 4 + 7*0.1 = 4.7; b[0] = 7 + 4 = 11; a[1] = 11 + 2*0.1 = 11.2
- i=1: a[1] = 5 + 8*0.2 = 6.6; b[1] = 8 + 5 = 13; a[2] = 13 + 3*0.2 = 13.6
Final: a = [4.7, 6.6, 13.6], b = [11, 13, 6]

**Optimized code execution**:
- i=0: a_next_old = a[1] = 2; b_new = 7+4=11; a[0]=4+7*0.1=4.7; b[0]=11; a[1]=11+2*0.1=11.2
- i=1: a_next_temp = 2 (old value); a_next_old = a[2]=3; b_new=8+5=13; a[1]=5+8*0.2=6.6; b[1]=13; a[2]=13+2*0.2=13.4
Final: a = [4.7, 6.6, 13.4], b = [11, 13, 6]

**Semantic difference**: a[2] is 13.6 in original but 13.4 in optimized code. The optimized code incorrectly uses the old a[1] value (2) instead of the current a[2] value (3) when computing a[2].

**Root cause**: The optimized code attempts to break the dependency by storing a[i+1] values, but this changes the semantics because a[i+1] is updated in iteration i-1, then read in iteration i. The original code has a true dependency: a[i+1] in iteration i uses the value computed in iteration i-1.

**Conclusion**: The optimized code has incorrect semantics. The attempt to break the dependency by storing old values fails because it doesn't preserve the flow of data through iterations.

**UNIT TEST OUTPUT**: The unit test indicates the output is inconsistent, confirming semantic mismatch.

### 3. Vectorization Analysis (Not Applicable)
Since the optimized code has incorrect semantics, vectorization analysis is not applicable.

### 4. Optimization Opportunity
The current optimization attempt introduces semantic errors. The original code has loop-carried dependencies that prevent straightforward vectorization:
- a[i+1] depends on b[i] from the same iteration
- b[i] is updated and used in the next iteration for a[i+1]

**FAIL**: The optimized code has incorrect semantics and cannot be considered optimized.

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```

   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

void s244(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a,float* b,float* c,float* d)
{
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D-1; ++i) {
            a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];
            b[i] = c[i] + b[i];
            a[i+1] = b[i] + a[i+1] * d[i];
        }
    }
}
```

void s244_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c, float* d) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Temporary array to store original a[i+1] values
        float a_next_orig[LEN_1D];
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D-1; ++i) {
            a_next_orig[i] = a[i+1];
        }
        
        // Compute a[i] and update b[i] for all i
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D-1; ++i) {
            a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];
            b[i] = c[i] + b[i];
        }
        
        // Compute a[i+1] using updated b[i] and original a[i+1]
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D-1; ++i) {
            a[i+1] = b[i] + a_next_orig[i] * d[i];
        }
    }
}

## Analysis  

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code

### 2. Semantic Comparison  

**Original loop body for each `i`:**  
```c
a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];
b[i] = c[i] + b[i];
a[i+1] = b[i] + a[i+1] * d[i];
```

**Key dependencies:**  
- `a[i]` depends on original `b[i]`, `c[i]`, `d[i]`.  
- `b[i]` is updated using original `b[i]` and `c[i]`.  
- `a[i+1]` uses **updated** `b[i]` and **original** `a[i+1]`.  

**Optimized code logic:**  
1. Save `a[i+1]` for all `i` into `a_next_orig[i]`.  
2. Compute `a[i]` and update `b[i]` for all `i`.  
3. Compute `a[i+1] = b[i] + a_next_orig[i] * d[i]`.  

**Problem:**  
In step 3, `a[i+1]` is computed using `b[i]` (already updated in step 2) and `a_next_orig[i]` (original `a[i+1]`).  
This matches the original semantics **only if** `a[i+1]` in the original code is the original value before the loop iteration for `i+1` begins.  

Let’s test with a small example:  

**Example 1:**  
`LEN_1D = 3`, initial values:  
```
a = [0,0,0]
b = [1,2,3]
c = [4,5,6]
d = [7,8,9]
```
**Original execution (`i=0`):**  
- `a[0] = 1 + 4*7 = 29`  
- `b[0] = 4 + 1 = 5`  
- `a[1] = 5 + 0*7 = 5`  

(`i=1`):  
- `a[1] = 2 + 5*8 = 42` (overwrites previous `a[1]=5`)  
- `b[1] = 5 + 2 = 7`  
- `a[2] = 7 + 0*8 = 7`  

Final `a = [29, 42, 7]`, `b = [5, 7, 3]`.  

**Optimized execution:**  
Step 1: `a_next_orig = [a[1], a[2]] = [0, 0]`.  
Step 2:  
- `a[0] = 1 + 4*7 = 29`, `b[0] = 5`  
- `a[1] = 2 + 5*8 = 42`, `b[1] = 7`  
Step 3:  
- `a[1] = b[0] + a_next_orig[0]*d[0] = 5 + 0*7 = 5` (overwrites `a[1]=42`!)  
- `a[2] = b[1] + a_next_orig[1]*d[1] = 7 + 0*8 = 7`  

Final `a = [29, 5, 7]`, `b = [5, 7, 3]`.  

**Difference:** `a[1]` is `5` in optimized vs `42` in original.  

**Reason:** In original, `a[1]` is computed twice:  
- First in iteration `i=0` as `a[1] = b[0] + a[1]*d[0]`.  
- Then in iteration `i=1` as `a[1] = b[1] + c[1]*d[1]`, **overwriting** previous value.  

But optimized code computes `a[1]` in step 2 as `b[1] + c[1]*d[1]`, then in step 3 overwrites it with `b[0] + orig_a[1]*d[0]`. This reverses the order of writes:  
Original: `a[1]` gets `b[0]+orig*d[0]` first, then `b[1]+c[1]*d[1]`.  
Optimized: `a[1]` gets `b[1]+c[1]*d[1]` first, then `b[0]+orig*d[0]`.  

Thus, semantics are **not preserved**.  

---

### 3. Conclusion  
The optimized code fails the semantic equivalence test.  
The transformation incorrectly reorders the writes to `a[i+1]`.  

**Output:** FAIL

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

Let’s go step by step.  

---

### **1. Compilation errors in the optimized code**
The optimized code declares a variable-length array:  
```c
float a_next_orig[LEN_1D];
```
If `LEN_1D` is large, this could cause a stack overflow, but it’s not a compilation error in C99/C11 with VLAs allowed.  
However, the compiler feedback shows `temp_code.cpp`, meaning it’s being compiled as C++ where VLAs are not standard (though some compilers support them as an extension).  
But the feedback says “vectorized loop,” so compilation succeeded here.  
So **no compilation errors** in this test environment.

---

### **2. Semantic comparison**

Original loop (for one iteration of `nl`):
```c
for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D-1; ++i) {
    a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];
    b[i] = c[i] + b[i];
    a[i+1] = b[i] + a[i+1] * d[i];
}
```
Important dependencies:  
- `a[i]` is written, then not read again in same iteration.  
- `b[i]` is updated (`b[i] = c[i] + b[i]`) after `a[i]` computation, before `a[i+1]` computation.  
- `a[i+1]` on RHS is **original** `a[i+1]` from start of current `nl` iteration (not the updated one from previous `i` in same `nl` iteration).  
- `a[i+1]` on LHS uses the **updated** `b[i]` from same `i`.

The optimized code tries to split into:  
1. Save `a[i+1]` for all `i` into `a_next_orig[i]`.  
2. Compute `a[i]` and update `b[i]` for all `i`.

But in step 3, `a[i+1] = b[i] + a_next_orig[i] * d[i]` uses `b[i]` from **after** step 2 update, which matches the original.  
So far it seems okay — but wait, check `a[i+1]` in original:  
Original: `a[i+1] = b[i] + a[i+1] * d[i]` where `a[i+1]` on RHS is the value at **start** of loop over `i` for current `nl`.

But there’s a subtlety:

When `i=1`, `a[2]` is computed using `b[1]` updated in same iteration, but `a[2]` on RHS is still original from start of `nl`.  
That’s exactly what optimized does: all `a[i+1]` RHS values are from original start of `nl`, all `b[i]` are updated before computing `a[i+1]`.  

So why does unit test say inconsistent?

        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D-1; ++i) {

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

Let’s go step by step.  

---

**1. Compilation errors in optimized code**  
The optimized code compiles (no syntax errors), but it has a runtime issue:  
- `a_next_orig = (float*)malloc(LEN_1D * sizeof(float));` allocates `LEN_1D` floats, but the loop `for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D-1; ++i)` writes to `a_next_orig[i]` where `i` goes up to `LEN_1D-2`.  
- This is fine for access, but `a_next_orig[LEN_1D-1]` is never set, though also never used.  
- The real problem is **semantic**, not compilation.

---

**2. Semantic comparison**  

Let’s trace the original loop for `i = 0` and `i = 1` in one iteration of `nl`:  

**Original code**:  
For `i = 0`:  
1. `a[0] = b[0] + c[0] * d[0];`  
2. `b[0] = c[0] + b[0];`  
3. `a[1] = b[0] + a[1] * d[0];`  

For `i = 1`:  
1. `a[1] = b[1] + c[1] * d[1];`  ← overwrites `a[1]` computed in step 3 of `i=0`  
2. `b[1] = c[1] + b[1];`  
3. `a[2] = b[1] + a[2] * d[1];`  

So `a[1]` is computed twice: first in iteration `i=0` step 3, then overwritten in iteration `i=1` step 1.  
The final `a[1]` after loop finishes is from `i=1` step 1, **not** from `i=0` step 3.  

---

**Optimized code**:  
First loop (`i = 0..LEN_1D-2`):  
1. `a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];`  
2. `b[i] = c[i] + b[i];`  

Second loop (`i = 0..LEN_1D-2`):  
`a[i+1] = b[i] + a_next_orig[i] * d[i];`  

Here `a_next_orig[i]` is the original `a[i+1]` before any modifications.  

But in the original code, when computing `a[i+1]` in the first loop (`i` iteration), the `a[i+1]` used is the **original** `a[i+1]` from before the loop (same as saved), **but** `b[i]` used is the **updated** `b[i]` from step 2 of same iteration.  

The optimized code’s second loop uses `b[i]` after update (correct), and `a_next_orig[i]` (original `a[i+1]`), so that matches the original for `i=0` step 3.  

However, in original code, `a[1]` computed in `i=0` step 3 is **overwritten** in `i=1` step 1.  

In optimized code:  
- First loop computes `a[1] = b[1] + c[1] * d[1];` (same as original `i=1` step 1).  
- Second loop computes `a[1] = b[0] + a_next_orig[0] * d[0];` (same as original `i=0` step 3).  

But the **order** matters:  
In optimized code, second loop runs **after** first loop, so `a[1]` from first loop (`i=1` step 1) is overwritten by second loop (`i=0` step 3).  

That’s **opposite** of original: in original, `i=0` step 3 happens before `i=1` step 1, so final `a[1]` is from `i=1` step 1.  

Thus semantics differ.  

---

**Test with small example**:  
Let `LEN_1D = 3`, initial:  
`a = [A0, A1, A2]`  
`b = [B0, B1, B2]`  
`c = [C0, C1, C2]`  
`d = [D0, D1, D2]`  

**Original**:  
i=0:  
a0 = B0 + C0*D0  
b0 = C0 + B0  
a1 = b0 + A1*D0  

i=1:  
a1 = B1 + C1*D1  
b1 = C1 + B1  
a2 = b1 + A2*D1  

Final a = [B0+C0*D0,  B1+C1*D1,  b1+A2*D1]  

**Optimized**:  
First loop (i=0..1):  
a0 = B0 + C0*D0  
b0 = C0 + B0  
a1 = B1 + C1*D1  
b1 = C1 + B1  

Second loop (i=0..1):  
a1 = b0 + A1*D0  
a2 = b1 + A2*D1  

Final a = [B0+C0*D0,  b0+A1*D0,  b1+A2*D1]  

Different! Indeed `a[1]` differs.

Optimized code:
```c

void s244_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c, float* d) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Save original a[i+1] values before any modifications
        float* a_next_orig = (float*)malloc(LEN_1D * sizeof(float));
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D-1; ++i) {
            a_next_orig[i] = a[i+1];
        }
        
        // Compute a[i] and update b[i] for all i
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D-1; ++i) {
            a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];
            b[i] = c[i] + b[i];
        }
        
        // Compute a[i+1] using updated b[i] and original a[i+1]
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D-1; ++i) {
            a[i+1] = b[i] + a_next_orig[i] * d[i];
        }
        
        free(a_next_orig);
    }
}

```

**1. Compilation errors in optimized code**

- This is fine for access, but `a_next_orig[LEN_1D-1]` is never set, though also never used.  
- The real problem is **semantic**, not compilation.

---

**2. Semantic comparison**  

Let’s trace the original loop for `i = 0` and `i = 1` in one iteration of `nl`:  

**Original code**:  
For `i = 0`:  
1. `a[0] = b[0] + c[0] * d[0];`  
2. `b[0] = c[0] + b[0];`  
3. `a[1] = b[0] + a[1] * d[0];`  

For `i = 1`:  
1. `a[1] = b[1] + c[1] * d[1];`  ← overwrites `a[1]` computed in step 3 of `i=0`  
2. `b[1] = c[1] + b[1];`  
3. `a[2] = b[1] + a[2] * d[1];`  

So `a[1]` is computed twice: first in iteration `i=0` step 3, then overwritten in iteration `i=1` step 1.  
The final `a[1]` after loop finishes is from `i=1` step 1, **not** from `i=0` step 3.  

---

**Optimized code**:  
First loop (`i = 0..LEN_1D-2`):  
1. `a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];`  
2. `b[i] = c[i] + b[i];`  

Second loop (`i = 0..LEN_1D-2`):  
`a[i+1] = b[i] + a_next_orig[i] * d[i];`  

Here `a_next_orig[i]` is the original `a[i+1]` before any modifications.  

But in the original code, when computing `a[i+1]` in the first loop (`i` iteration), the `a[i+1]` used is the **original** `a[i+1]` from before the loop (same as saved), **but** `b[i]` used is the **updated** `b[i]` from step 2 of same iteration.  

The optimized code’s second loop uses `b[i]` after update (correct), and `a_next_orig[i]` (original `a[i+1]`), so that matches the original for `i=0` step 3.  

However, in original code, `a[1]` computed in `i=0` step 3 is **overwritten** in `i=1` step 1.  

In optimized code:  
- First loop computes `a[1] = b[1] + c[1] * d[1];` (same as original `i=1` step 1).  
- Second loop computes `a[1] = b[0] + a_next_orig[0] * d[0];` (same as original `i=0` step 3).  

But the **order** matters:  
In optimized code, second loop runs **after** first loop, so `a[1]` from first loop (`i=1` step 1) is overwritten by second loop (`i=0` step 3).  

That’s **opposite** of original: in original, `i=0` step 3 happens before `i=1` step 1, so final `a[1]` is from `i=1` step 1.  

Thus semantics differ.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.

    // Original

    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.

    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

void s244(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a,float* b,float* c,float* d)
{
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D-1; ++i) {
            a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];

Optimized code:
```c

void s244_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c, float* d) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Temporary array to store original a[i+1] values
        float* a_next_orig = (float*)malloc((LEN_1D - 1) * sizeof(float));
        
        // First loop: capture original a[i+1] and compute new a[i], b[i]
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D - 1; ++i) {
            a_next_orig[i] = a[i + 1];
            a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];
            b[i] = c[i] + b[i];
        }
        
        // Second loop: compute new a[i+1] using updated b[i] and original a[i+1]
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D - 1; ++i) {
            a[i + 1] = b[i] + a_next_orig[i] * d[i];
        }
        
        free(a_next_orig);
    }
}

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

void s244(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a,float* b,float* c,float* d)
{
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D-1; ++i) {
            a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];

}

Optimized code:
```c

void s244_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c, float* d) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Store original a[i+1] values before any modification
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D - 1; ++i) {
            float temp_a_next = a[i + 1];
            // Compute new a[i] and b[i]
            a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];
            b[i] = c[i] + b[i];
            // Use stored original a[i+1] to compute new a[i+1]
            a[i + 1] = b[i] + temp_a_next * d[i];
        }
    }
}

```

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }

   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.

     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

void s244_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c, float* d) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Store original a[i+1] values before any modifications
        float* a_next = (float*)malloc((LEN_1D - 1) * sizeof(float));
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D - 1; ++i) {
            a_next[i] = a[i + 1];

            a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];

            a[i + 1] = b[i] + a_next[i] * d[i];

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

{
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {

Optimized code:
```c

void s244_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c, float* d) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Store original a[i+1] values for the first iteration's use
        float* a_next = (float*)malloc((LEN_1D - 1) * sizeof(float));
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D - 1; ++i) {
            a_next[i] = a[i + 1];
        }
        
        // Phase 1: Compute a[i] using original b[i]
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D - 1; ++i) {
            a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];
        }
        
        // Phase 2: Update b[i]
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D - 1; ++i) {
            b[i] = c[i] + b[i];
        }
        
        // Phase 3: Compute a[i+1] using updated b[i] and stored original a[i+1]
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D - 1; ++i) {
            a[i + 1] = b[i] + a_next[i] * d[i];
        }
        
        free(a_next);
    }
}

```

## **2. Semantic comparison**

The original loop in `s244` for a given `i` does:

1. `a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];`
2. `b[i] = c[i] + b[i];`
3. `a[i+1] = b[i] + a[i+1] * d[i];`

Important: Step 3 uses **the updated `b[i]`** (after step 2) and **the original `a[i+1]`** from before the iteration started.

The optimized code tries to split into three separate loops:

- Loop 1: compute `a[i]` using original `b[i]`.
- Loop 2: update `b[i]` to `c[i] + b[i]`.
- Loop 3: compute `a[i+1]` using updated `b[i]` and stored original `a[i+1]`.

But there’s a **critical flaw**:  
In the original code, `a[i+1]` in step 3 is the **original** `a[i+1]` from before **any updates in this iteration of `nl`**.  
However, in the optimized code, `a[i+1]` is stored **before** computing `a[i]` in the same `nl` iteration — that’s correct for the first `i` but not for later `i` in the same `nl` iteration? Wait, let’s check carefully.

Actually, in the original loop, `a[i+1]` in step 3 could have been overwritten in the **previous iteration** when `i` was smaller. Let’s test with a tiny example.

---

### **Example 1**: LEN_1D = 3, iterations = 1

Initial:
```
a = [a0, a1, a2]
b = [b0, b1, b2]
c = [c0, c1, c2]
d = [d0, d1, d2]
```

**Original code**:

i=0:
1. `a[0] = b0 + c0*d0`
2. `b[0] = c0 + b0`
3. `a[1] = b[0] + a1 * d0`  ← uses original a1

i=1:
1. `a[1] = b1 + c1*d1`  ← overwrites a[1] just computed in i=0!
2. `b[1] = c1 + b1`
3. `a[2] = b[1] + a2 * d1`

So `a[1]` is computed twice in the same `nl` iteration — first in step 3 of i=0, then in step 1 of i=1.  
The second computation uses **original b1** (not updated yet in this i=1 iteration) and original c1, d1.

---

**Optimized code**:

- Stores `a_next[i] = a[i+1]` for i=0..1:  
  `a_next[0] = a1`, `a_next[1] = a2`.

- Loop1: `a[0] = b0 + c0*d0`, `a[1] = b1 + c1*d1`.

- Loop2: `b[0] = c0 + b0`, `b[1] = c1 + b1`.

- Loop3:  
  `a[1] = b[0] + a_next[0]*d0` = `b[0] + a1*d0`  
  `a[2] = b[1] + a_next[1]*d1` = `b[1] + a2*d1`

Compare final `a[1]`:

Original: `a[1]` after i=0 = `b[0]_updated + a1*d0`. Then i=1 overwrites it with `b1 + c1*d1`.  
So final `a[1] = b1 + c1*d1`.

Optimized: final `a[1] = b[0]_updated + a1*d0`.  
These are different unless `b1 + c1*d1 = b[0]_updated + a1*d0` in general, which is false.

Thus **semantics differ**.

---

The optimized code incorrectly preserves `a[i+1]` from **before the outer iteration** for all `i`, but in the original code, `a[i+1]` used in step 3 for iteration `i` is the **original** from before **this nl iteration**, yes — but for `i>0`, that `a[i+1]` has **not** been overwritten earlier in the same nl iteration except by step 3 of iteration `i-1`. Wait — step 3 of iteration `i-1` **does** overwrite `a[i]` (which is `a[i+1]` relative to previous iteration). So `a[i+1]` for current i is still original from start of nl iteration.

Thus storing original `a[i+1]` before any updates in the nl iteration is correct.

But the problem: In original, `a[i]` for i>0 is computed in step 1 using original `b[i]`, then `b[i]` updated, then `a[i+1]` computed using updated `b[i]` and original `a[i+1]`.  
In optimized, Loop1 computes `a[i]` for all i using original `b[i]`. That’s fine. Loop2 updates all `b[i]`. Loop3 computes `a[i+1]` for all i using updated `b[i]` and original `a[i+1]`.  
That matches original **if** in original, computing `a[i+1]` in step 3 of iteration i uses `b[i]` updated in step 2 of same i — yes.

But in original, `a[i+1]` computed in step 3 of iteration i is **overwritten** in step 1 of iteration i+1 (except for last i). So final `a[i]` for i=1..LEN_1D-2 is **not** from step 3 of previous iteration, but from step 1 of same index iteration.

Thus in original:
- Final `a[0]` = `b0_orig + c0*d0`
- Final `a[i]` for i=1..LEN_1D-2 = `b[i]_orig + c[i]*d[i]`
- Final `a[LEN_1D-1]` = `b[LEN_1D-2]_updated + a[LEN_1D-1]_orig * d[LEN_1D-2]`

In optimized:
- Final `a[0]` = `b0_orig + c0*d0`
- Final `a[i]` for i=1..LEN_1D-2 = `b[i-1]_updated + a[i]_orig * d[i-1]`
- Final `a[LEN_1D-1]` = `b[LEN_1D-2]_updated + a[LEN_1D-1]_orig * d[LEN_1D-2]`

These differ for i=1..LEN_1D-2. So semantics are wrong.

---

**Conclusion**: The optimized code changes the semantics.  
The unit test analysis is correct: semantics are inconsistent.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

**2. Semantic comparison**

Original loop (for one iteration of `nl`):

```c
for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D-1; ++i) {
    a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];        // (1)
    b[i] = c[i] + b[i];               // (2)
    a[i+1] = b[i] + a[i+1] * d[i];    // (3)
}
```

Important dependencies:  
- In (1), `a[i]` uses original `b[i]` and `c[i]`, `d[i]`.  
- In (2), `b[i]` is updated using original `b[i]` and `c[i]`.  
- In (3), `a[i+1]` uses **updated** `b[i]` (from (2)) and **original** `a[i+1]` (from before the loop iteration `i`), and `d[i]`.

The tricky part: `a[i+1]` in (3) is the original `a[i+1]` from before the whole inner loop started (for this `nl` iteration), **not** the value computed in a previous iteration of this loop for `i-1` (which would be `a[i]`).  
Wait — check: When `i=0`, `a[1]` in (3) is original `a[1]` before loop starts. When `i=1`, `a[2]` in (3) is original `a[2]` before loop starts, **not** the value written at `i=0` (which was `a[1]`).  
So indeed, each `a[i+1]` is computed from original `a[i+1]` and `b[i]` after (2). There’s no loop-carried dependence on `a` across iterations except that `a[i]` is written in (1) and never read later in same iteration — so safe.

Now check optimized version:  
It stores original `a[i+1]` for `i=0..LEN_1D-2` into `a_next`.  
Phase 1: `a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i]` — same as (1).  
Phase 2: `b[i] = c[i] + b[i]` — same as (2).  
Phase 3: `a[i+1] = b[i] + a_next[i] * d[i]` — same as (3) because `a_next[i]` is original `a[i+1]`.

This seems correct — but wait: In original, when computing `a[i+1]` in (3), `b[i]` is the updated `b[i]` from (2) in same iteration `i`. That’s preserved in optimized version because Phase 2 updates `b[i]` before Phase 3 uses it.

So why does unit test say semantics inconsistent?  
Let’s test small example manually.

Take `LEN_1D = 3`, `iterations = 1`, initial:

```
a = [a0, a1, a2]
b = [b0, b1, b2]
c = [c0, c1, c2]
d = [d0, d1, d2]
```

**Original execution** (`i=0..1`):

i=0:  
(1) `a[0] = b0 + c0*d0`  
(2) `b[0] = c0 + b0`  
(3) `a[1] = b[0] + a1*d0`  // uses original a1

i=1:  
(1) `a[1] = b1 + c1*d1`  // **overwrites** a[1] from previous step!  
(2) `b[1] = c1 + b1`  
(3) `a[2] = b[1] + a2*d1`  // uses original a2

So final `a[1] = b1 + c1*d1`, not `b[0] + a1*d0`.  
The original code’s (3) writes to `a[i+1]` but that value is overwritten in next iteration’s (1) except for last `i`.

Thus original’s net effect for `i=0..LEN_1D-2`:  
- `a[i] = b[i] + c[i]*d[i]` for all i=0..LEN_1D-2  
- `b[i] = c[i] + b[i]` for all i=0..LEN_1D-2  
- `a[LEN_1D-1] = b[LEN_1D-2] + a[LEN_1D-1]*d[LEN_1D-2]` (only last a[i+1] survives)

Check: For LEN_1D=3, final:
a[0] = b0 + c0*d0  
a[1] = b1 + c1*d1  
a[2] = b[1] + a2*d1  (b[1] updated to c1+b1)

b[1] = c1+b1  
b[2] unchanged.

**Optimized version**:  
Phase 1: a[0]=b0+c0*d0, a[1]=b1+c1*d1  
Phase 2: b[0]=c0+b0, b[1]=c1+b1  
Phase 3: a[1]=b[0]+a1*d0, a[2]=b[1]+a2*d1  

So final a[1] = b[0]+a1*d0, not b1+c1*d1.  
Mismatch! Because Phase 3 overwrites a[1] after Phase 1 wrote it, but original overwrites a[1] in next iteration’s Phase 1-equivalent step, which happens after (3) for i=0.

Thus optimized version is wrong: it should not compute all Phase 1 for all i first, because a[i] for i>=1 is overwritten later in original loop’s (3) for i-1? Wait, careful:

Actually in original:  
i=0: writes a[0] in (1), a[1] in (3)

So only a[0] and a[2] survive? No, a[1] final = from i=1’s (1).  
So indeed, in original, a[i] for i=1..LEN_1D-2 comes from (1) at same i, not from (3) at i-1.

Thus optimized version’s Phase 3 should only compute a[LEN_1D-1] = b[LEN_1D-2] + original_a[LEN_1D-1] * d[LEN_1D-2].  
Others a[i+1] for i<LEN_1D-2 are overwritten later anyway, so don’t compute them in Phase 3.

So optimized code’s Phase 3 is wrong for all i except i = LEN_1D-2.

#include <string.h>

void s244_opt(int iterations, int LEN_1D, float* a, float* b, float* c, float* d) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations; nl++) {
        // Store original a[i+1] values for all i
        float* a_next = (float*)malloc((LEN_1D - 1) * sizeof(float));
        memcpy(a_next, a + 1, (LEN_1D - 1) * sizeof(float));
        
        // Phase 1: Compute a[i] using original b[i]
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D - 1; ++i) {
            a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];
        }
        
        // Phase 2: Update b[i]
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D - 1; ++i) {
            b[i] = c[i] + b[i];
        }
        
        // Phase 3: Compute final a[i+1] using updated b[i] and stored original a[i+1]
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D - 1; ++i) {
            a[i + 1] = b[i] + a_next[i] * d[i];
        }
        
        free(a_next);
    }
}

```

**1. Compilation errors in optimized code**

**2. Semantic comparison**

Original loop (for one iteration of `nl`):

```c
for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D-1; ++i) {
    a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i];        // (1)
    b[i] = c[i] + b[i];               // (2)
    a[i+1] = b[i] + a[i+1] * d[i];    // (3)
}
```

Important dependencies:  
- In (1), `a[i]` uses original `b[i]` and `c[i]`, `d[i]`.  
- In (2), `b[i]` is updated using original `b[i]` and `c[i]`.  
- In (3), `a[i+1]` uses **updated** `b[i]` (from (2)) and **original** `a[i+1]` (from before the loop iteration `i`), and `d[i]`.

The tricky part: `a[i+1]` in (3) is the original `a[i+1]` from before the whole inner loop started (for this `nl` iteration), **not** the value computed in a previous iteration of this loop for `i-1` (which would be `a[i]`).  
Wait — check: When `i=0`, `a[1]` in (3) is original `a[1]` before loop starts. When `i=1`, `a[2]` in (3) is original `a[2]` before loop starts, **not** the value written at `i=0` (which was `a[1]`).  
So indeed, each `a[i+1]` is computed from original `a[i+1]` and `b[i]` after (2). There’s no loop-carried dependence on `a` across iterations except that `a[i]` is written in (1) and never read later in same iteration — so safe.

Now check optimized version:  
It stores original `a[i+1]` for `i=0..LEN_1D-2` into `a_next`.  
Phase 1: `a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i]` — same as (1).  
Phase 2: `b[i] = c[i] + b[i]` — same as (2).  
Phase 3: `a[i+1] = b[i] + a_next[i] * d[i]` — same as (3) because `a_next[i]` is original `a[i+1]`.

This seems correct — but wait: In original, when computing `a[i+1]` in (3), `b[i]` is the updated `b[i]` from (2) in same iteration `i`. That’s preserved in optimized version because Phase 2 updates `b[i]` before Phase 3 uses it.

So why does unit test say semantics inconsistent?  
Let’s test small example manually.

Take `LEN_1D = 3`, `iterations = 1`, initial:

```
a = [a0, a1, a2]
b = [b0, b1, b2]
c = [c0, c1, c2]
d = [d0, d1, d2]
```

**Original execution** (`i=0..1`):

i=0:  
(1) `a[0] = b0 + c0*d0`  
(2) `b[0] = c0 + b0`  
(3) `a[1] = b[0] + a1*d0`  // uses original a1

i=1:  
(1) `a[1] = b1 + c1*d1`  // **overwrites** a[1] from previous step!  
(2) `b[1] = c1 + b1`  
(3) `a[2] = b[1] + a2*d1`  // uses original a2

So final `a[1] = b1 + c1*d1`, not `b[0] + a1*d0`.  
The original code’s (3) writes to `a[i+1]` but that value is overwritten in next iteration’s (1) except for last `i`.

Thus original’s net effect for `i=0..LEN_1D-2`:  
- `a[i] = b[i] + c[i]*d[i]` for all i=0..LEN_1D-2  
- `b[i] = c[i] + b[i]` for all i=0..LEN_1D-2  
- `a[LEN_1D-1] = b[LEN_1D-2] + a[LEN_1D-1]*d[LEN_1D-2]` (only last a[i+1] survives)

Check: For LEN_1D=3, final:
a[0] = b0 + c0*d0  
a[1] = b1 + c1*d1  
a[2] = b[1] + a2*d1  (b[1] updated to c1+b1)  
b[0] = c0+b0  
b[1] = c1+b1  
b[2] unchanged.

**Optimized version**:  
Phase 1: a[0]=b0+c0*d0, a[1]=b1+c1*d1  
Phase 2: b[0]=c0+b0, b[1]=c1+b1  
Phase 3: a[1]=b[0]+a1*d0, a[2]=b[1]+a2*d1  

So final a[1] = b[0]+a1*d0, not b1+c1*d1.  
Mismatch! Because Phase 3 overwrites a[1] after Phase 1 wrote it, but original overwrites a[1] in next iteration’s Phase 1-equivalent step, which happens after (3) for i=0.

Thus optimized version is wrong: it should not compute all Phase 1 for all i first, because a[i] for i>=1 is overwritten later in original loop’s (3) for i-1? Wait, careful:

Actually in original:  
i=0: writes a[0] in (1), a[1] in (3)  
i=1: writes a[1] in (1) (overwrites previous a[1]), a[2] in (3)

So only a[0] and a[2] survive? No, a[1] final = from i=1’s (1).  
So indeed, in original, a[i] for i=1..LEN_1D-2 comes from (1) at same i, not from (3) at i-1.

Thus optimized version’s Phase 3 should only compute a[LEN_1D-1] = b[LEN_1D-2] + original_a[LEN_1D-1] * d[LEN_1D-2].  
Others a[i+1] for i<LEN_1D-2 are overwritten later anyway, so don’t compute them in Phase 3.

So optimized code’s Phase 3 is wrong for all i except i = LEN_1D-2.

---

**3. Conclusion**

Semantics are indeed inconsistent. The optimized code incorrectly preserves intermediate `a[i+1]` computations that are overwritten in the original loop.

Thus we **FAIL** at semantic comparison stage.

FAIL

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases
**The optimized code has incorrect semantics.** Let's analyze step by step:

**Original code logic:**
For each iteration `nl`, the loop processes:
1. `a[i] = b[i] + c[i] * d[i]` (using original `b[i]`)
2. `b[i] = c[i] + b[i]` (updates `b[i]` in-place)
3. `a[i+1] = b[i] + a[i+1] * d[i]` (uses **updated** `b[i]` from step 2)

**Key dependency:** The calculation of `a[i+1]` uses the **updated** `b[i]` value from the same iteration.

**Optimized code issues:**
1. Phase 1 computes all `a[i]` using original `b[i]` values - this is correct.
2. Phase 2 updates all `b[i]` values - this is correct.
3. Phase 3 only computes `a[LEN_1D-1]`, but the original code computes `a[i+1]` for **all** `i` from 0 to `LEN_1D-2`.

**Example verification (LEN_1D=3, iterations=1):**
Original execution:
- i=0: a[0]=b0+c0*d0, b0=c0+b0, a[1]=b0+a1*d0
- i=1: a[1]=b1+c1*d1, b1=c1+b1, a[2]=b1+a2*d1

Optimized execution:
- Phase 1: a[0]=b0+c0*d0, a[1]=b1+c1*d1
- Phase 2: b0=c0+b0, b1=c1+b1  
- Phase 3: a[2]=b1+a2*d1

**Problem:** In optimized code, `a[1]` is computed twice (in Phase 1 and should be in Phase 3 but isn't), and the second computation using updated `b[0]` is missing.

**Conclusion:** The optimized code fails to compute intermediate `a[i+1]` values correctly for `i=0..LEN_1D-3`. Only `a[LEN_1D-1]` is handled correctly.

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

        }
    }
}

This is a variable-length array (VLA), which is valid in C99 but may fail if `LEN_1D` is very large (stack overflow) or if the compiler is in strict C89 mode.  
However, the feedback shows the compiler compiled it without errors, so we proceed.
